## 1. [The impact of Population III stars on the elemental enrichment of ultra-faint galaxies in the FIRE-3 zoom-in simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05589) <a id="link1"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05589

**Authors:** Pratik J. Gandhi, Earl P. Bellinger, Marla Geha, Andrew Wetzel, Coral Wheeler, Yasmine J. Meziani, Jenna Samuel, Francisco J. Mercado, Jorge Moreno, Philip F. Hopkins

**Abstract:** The observed stellar mass-metallicity relation for ultra-faint galaxies in the Local Group (LG) is in tension with many theoretical predictions, suggesting the need for modified or additional channels of elemental enrichment at low metallicities. We present a suite of FIRE-3 cosmological baryonic zoom-in simulations of isolated ultra-faint galaxies run to z=0 (maximum baryonic resolution ~ 30 Msun, 0.1 pc) and LMC-mass environments (maximum baryonic resolution ~ 110 Msun, 0.5 pc), with different sub-grid models for Population III stars. Under our Pop. III model(s), star particles forming below a critical metallicity Z_crit represent an initial mass function (IMF) consisting of only massive stars (8-100 Msun). We examine how various stellar feedback processes impact the star formation histories (SFHs) and elemental abundances of ultra-faint galaxies. We find that our Pop. III model variants introduce an effective metallicity floor in ultra-faints at z=0, at Z_floor ~ 1e-5 Zsun to 1e-3 Zsun or [Fe/H]_floor ~ -7 to -5 depending on the model. However, simply including the Pop. III model does not systematically guarantee that all (or even most) ultra-faints enrich up to [Fe/H] ~ -2.5 to fully resolve the tension with the observed mass-metallicity relation, even though we obtain some galaxies at that level. Crucially, the ultra-faints that do enrich in [Fe/H] to this level observed in the LG have extended SFHs after Pop. III enrichment and significant iron enrichment from white dwarf (Type Ia) supernovae, indicating that iron enrichment from Pop. III alone is not sufficient. We also examine the ways in which early-time Pop. III feedback impacts the metallicity distributions and [Mg/Fe] abundance ratios in ultra-faint galaxies, and make a prediction for the stellar mass - [Mg/H] metallicity relation from our simulations.

**Comment:** FIRE-3 simulations of ultra-faint galaxies, SFHs, abundances

**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 2. [She Sees Shells by the Stellar Shore: A Kinematic View of NGC 253's Stellar Halo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05493) <a id="link2"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05493

**Authors:** Katya Gozman, Eric F. Bell, Richard D'Souza, Mario Mateo, John I. Bailey III, Antonela Monachesi, Camila Beltrand

**Abstract:** The stellar halos of nearby galaxies are fossil records of ancient mergers, providing insight into the formation and growth of these systems. These halos often contain substructure in the form of streams, shells, and debris fields that make up dynamically young remnants of past accretions. These remnants can be probed with resolved-star spectroscopic observations, giving insight into the kinematics of bygone satellite companions. Such observations have been made for the Milky Way (MW) and Andromeda, but are much more challenging and expensive to do outside the Local Group. The Local Volume has an abundance of MW-mass galaxies with richly structured halos, such as NGC 253. Leveraging the power of Magellan's M2FS fiber-fed multiobject spectrograph, we have obtained medium-resolution spectra of 225 stars centered on NGC 253's southwestern shell. These spectra enabled us to make the first-ever measurement of the resolved-star stellar kinematics for 89 stars in NGC 253's halo. We also detect substructure in several ways and identify regions likely to be accreted and in-situ, finding among them a low-velocity region coincident with the previously reported SW shell we believe was accreted recently, and an in-situ component that traces the outer disk. After subtracting NGC 253's systemic velocity, we measure median line-of-sight velocities of $-34_{-12}^{+16}$ km/s and $167_{-14}^{+16}$ km/s for the accreted and in-situ populations, respectively. This work highlights the utility of kinematics to differentiate between accreted and in-situ stars, and acts as a pathfinder for future observations with next-generation 30 m telescopes and wide-field multiobject spectrographs.

**Comment:** Accreted stellar halo substructure, near-field cosmology

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 3. [Constraining the Milky Way Mass profile with data driven distribution function using DESI DR2 halo stars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05520) <a id="link3"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05520

**Authors:** Yanrui Zhou, Jiaxin Han, Wenting Wang, Zhaozhou Li, Alexander H. Riley, Monica Valluri, Amanda Byström, Gustavo E. Medina, Sergey E. Koposov, Ting S. Li, Leandro Beraldo e Silva, Oleg Y. Gnedin, N. Kizhuprakkat, N. Sandford, C. S. Frenk, Binbin Gao, Yiran Wei, J. Aguilar, S. Ahlen, C. Allende Prieto, F. Beutler, D. Bianchi, D. Brooks, A. Carnero Rosell, T. Claybaugh, A. P. Cooper, A. de la Macorra, Arjun Dey, Biprateep Dey, J. E. Forero-Romero, E. Gaztañaga, Satya Gontcho A Gontcho, G. Gutierrez, M. Ishak, S. Juneau, T. Karim, R. Kehoe, O. Lahav, A. Lambert, M. Landriau, L. Le Guillou, A. Leauthaud, M. E. Levi, A. Meisner, R. Miquel, S. Nadathur, E. Paillas, N. Palanque-Delabrouille, W. J. Percival, F. Prada, I. Pérez-Ràfols, C. Ravoux, G. Rossi, E. Sanchez, C. Saulder, D. Schlegel, J. Silber, M. Siudek, G. Tarlé, B. A. Weaver

**Abstract:** We employ a novel data-driven dynamical modeling method, the empirical distribution function (emPDF), to constrain the Milky Way mass profile using a large sample of halo stars from the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument Milky Way Survey (DESI MWS). The method does not assume any functional form of the tracer distribution. Deviations from the steady-state assumption and the effects of observational errors are further tested and calibrated using a number of realistic mock observations (AuriDESI). Applying the calibrated method to two classes of tracers in DESI, we derive a best-constrained virial mass and concentration of $\log M_\mathrm{200c}/M_{\odot}=12.05_{-0.12}^{+0.21}(_{-0.18}^{+0.26})$ and $\log c_\mathrm{200c}=1.20_{-0.16}^{+0.11}(_{-0.24}^{+0.20})$ for BHBs. For RR Lyrae stars we derive $\log M_\mathrm{200c}/M_{\odot}=12.15_{-0.14}^{+0.22}(_{-0.19}^{+0.26})$ and $\log c_\mathrm{200c}=1.16_{-0.12}^{+0.09}(_{-0.18}^{+0.16})$. The uncertainties quoted outside and within brackets are statistical and total errors including systematic errors calibrated from AuriDESI, respectively. The two tracer populations provide overall consistent measurements of the MW halo mass profile. Combining the halo star constraints with the independent constraint from satellite dynamics, we derive a robust and precise final measurement of the MW halo parameters of $\log M_\mathrm{200c}/M_{\odot}=12.12_{-0.05}^{+0.05}(_{-0.04}^{+0.06})$ and $\log c_\mathrm{200c}=1.16_{-0.03}^{+0.05}(_{-0.07}^{+0.05})$, with correlation coefficients of $\rho=-0.90(-0.54)$.

**Comment:** Milky Way mass profile, near-field cosmology

## 4. [The formation and evolution of Wolf-Rayet stars with temperature-dependent winds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06650) <a id="link4"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06650

**Authors:** Guy O. Flint, Avishai Gilkis, Tomer Shenar, Christopher A. Tout

**Abstract:** Massive stars strongly influence their environments through their radiation, stellar winds and core-collapse supernovae, while their own evolution is strongly shaped by wind mass loss. Wolf-Rayet (WR) stars are characterised by strong, optically thick winds, and the implementation of these winds remains an important uncertainty in stellar evolution models. Recent hydrodynamically consistent atmosphere calculations predict WR mass-loss rates that depend strongly on the temperature at the wind-launching region. We investigate the effects of incorporating these latest theoretical WR wind prescriptions into single and binary stellar evolution models at Milky Way, Large Magellanic Cloud and Small Magellanic Cloud (SMC) metallicities. We find that the temperature dependence of the WR wind can significantly alter the evolution of stars near the threshold for WR formation. In particular, an implementation that transitions from a hydrogen rich WNL wind prescription to a classical WR wind driven by the hot iron opacity bump causes some SMC-metallicity models to spend an extended period at temperatures of approximately 70 to 90 kK. This produces a concentration of models in the temperature range occupied by most observed SMC WR stars, a feature that has previously been difficult to explain with stellar evolution models. Our results demonstrate that incorporating physically motivated, temperature dependent WR winds can improve the agreement between stellar evolution models and observed WR populations.

**Comment:** New stellar evolutionary models with temperature-dependent WR winds

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 5. [The dwarf galaxy NGC 185: the flagship of Andromeda's dwarf galaxies for the study of evolved multiple stellar populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06547) <a id="link5"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06547

**Authors:** Paolo Ventura, Chiara Ventura, Richard D'Souza, Flavia Dell'Agli, Matteo Correnti, Claudio Gavetti, Marco Tailo, Miriam Di Stefano, Eric Bell

**Abstract:** Reaching galaxies at 10-15 Mpc with HST and JWST requires star   formation history (SFH) diagnostics that do not rely on the faint   main sequence turn-off. We investigate whether horizontal branch (HB),   red clump (RC), and red and asymptotic giant branch (RGB, AGB) stars can   fill this role for dwarf elliptical galaxies, using NGC 185 as a test case.   We use stellar evolution models of low- and intermediate-mass stars as inputs   to population synthesis simulations that reproduce the colour distribution and   luminosity function of stars in the (mF606W-mF814W, mF814W)   diagram. The study of the stars populating the HB is used to derive   the distance modulus and reddening, the average mass loss suffered by the stars during   the ascending on the RGB and the SFH occurred during the ancient epochs, before 7 Gyr ago.   The luminosity function of the RC gives us constraints on the recent SFH (<8 Gyr).   The distribution of HB stars yields a reddening E(B-V)=0.20 mag and a distance   modulus DM=24.10 mag for NGC 185. The average mass lost by low-mass stars during   the RGB phase is 0.22 +- 0.01 Msun. Star formation   was concentrated at early epochs, with half of the stars formed more than 11 Gyr ago.   The morphology of the HB indicates essentially no metallicity spread among stars older   than 8 Gyr, whereas the slope of the RGB suggests that part of the stars younger than   5-6 Gyr are slightly enriched in metals. Once RGB mass loss is adequately constrained, evolved stars can provide a   reliable SFH for dwarf elliptical galaxies well beyond the reach of MSTO-based methods.   We discuss the implications for more distant systems and the remaining uncertainties in   the underlying stellar models.

**Comment:** Resolved star formation history of dwarf elliptical via evolved stars

**Abstract:** We present measurements of the Milky Way mass using blue horizontal-branch (BHB) and RR Lyrae (RRL) stars from the second data release of DESI (DR2), covering its first three years of operations. We apply the Galactic Mass Estimator (GME), a hierarchical Bayesian framework that assumes power-law forms for the potential and the tracer density, and accounts for distance and velocity uncertainties. Validating the method on 24 AuriDESI mock catalogs (six Auriga halos at four viewing angles), we find that it overestimates the enclosed mass by $\sim\!15\%$ at 80\,kpc, and correct our results accordingly. We obtain $M(<80\,{\rm kpc}) = 0.64^{+0.06}_{-0.06}$ and $0.53^{+0.05}_{-0.05} \times 10^{12}\,M_\odot$ for BHBs and RRLs, respectively, while including Sagittarius stream stars lowers these masses by $\sim\!10$\%. Extrapolating to 100\,kpc, we obtain $M(<100\,{\rm kpc}) = 0.74^{+0.07}_{-0.07}$ and $0.62^{+0.06}_{-0.07} \times 10^{12}\,M_\odot$, and anchoring an NFW halo to our GME constraints at 65\,kpc yields virial masses of $M_{200} = 1.12^{+0.14}_{-0.12}$ and $0.89^{+0.11}_{-0.10} \times 10^{12}\,M_\odot$. BHBs yield systematically higher masses than RRLs across the full radial range, reflecting the impact of tracer choice even within a single method. This work is part of a wider DESI DR2 Key Project effort to measure the Milky Way mass and constrain systematics arising from different samples and methodologies.

## 9. [Mock CSST NUV-NIR imaging of TNG50 galaxies with resolved stellar populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05875) <a id="link9"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05875

**Authors:** Jing Tang, Ran Li, Yang Chen, Chao Liu, Alessandro Bressan, Léo Girardi, Chengliang Wei, Tianmeng Zhang

**Abstract:** Aims. We aim to develop a framework for producing CSST-like mock images of resolved stellar populations from simulated galaxies, demonstrated here using two TNG50 galaxies as illustrative test cases. Methods. Starting from simulated galaxies from TNG50, we use the population synthesis code TRILEGAL to sample individual stars from star particles, retaining bright stars explicitly and treating the remaining unresolved light, including faint stellar light and nebular emission from star-forming regions, as a diffuse background component of the galaxy. Based on the dust distribution inferred from the gas mass and metallicity of gas cells, we use the radiative transfer (RT) code SKIRT to construct the dust model. From this model, we compute the line-of-sight extinction for individual stars, while image-level transmission maps for the diffuse light are derived through RT simulations. For young stars still embedded in their natal molecular clouds, we additionally include birth-cloud attenuation. The resulting dust-attenuated stellar catalogues and diffuse-light images are then processed with the CSST simulation pipeline, which applies PSF convolution, sky background, and instrumental noise. Results. Our framework produces mock CSST images for both a spiral galaxy and a dwarf irregular galaxy from TNG50 in the seven photometric bands. The mock images combine resolved stars, unresolved diffuse light, and dust attenuation within a unified framework, and include the main observational effects expected for the survey. The images are provided with known astrometric and photometric calibration information, and are intended as realistic image-level inputs for subsequent source detection, photometric recovery, and completeness analyses of resolved stellar populations. The framework is designed to be adaptable to other hydrodynamic simulations and observing configurations.

**Comment:** Mock resolved stellar population imaging of simulated TNG50 galaxies

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 10. [Net Cooling Tables for Cosmological Simulations under Multiple UV/X-ray Background Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06360) <a id="link10"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06360

**Authors:** Laura Di Federico, Klaus Dolag, Ildar Khabibullin, Luca Sala, Geray S. Karademir, Milena Valentini

**Abstract:** We present three sets of net cooling tables for cosmological hydrodynamical simulations, computed with CLOUDY 23.01 for optically thin gas in photoionization equilibrium with three different UV/X-ray background models. Following the element-by-element approach, and in a format compatible with existing tables, we tabulate cooling, heating, and net rates separately for hydrogen and helium over an extended range of helium mass fractions and for 13 heavy elements, together with hydrogen species fractions, electron abundances, and mean particle masses. For gas of Solar composition, the choice of UV/X-ray background has little impact on the net cooling rates at low redshift, but the results increasingly diverge towards high redshift, particularly in diffuse gas near thermal equilibrium. Compared with widely used previous tables, cooling times in this regime differ by up to an order of magnitude at $z \gtrsim 4.6$, and the calculations disagree on whether the gas undergoes net cooling or heating over nearly 10% of the diffuse density-temperature plane at $z \approx 8$, corresponding to the intergalactic medium. These trends persist for gas composed exclusively of hydrogen and helium. The tables provide a consistent framework for assessing the impact of the radiation background on gas cooling in cosmological simulations.

**Comment:** Cooling tables for cosmological galaxy simulations

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 11. [Dynamics of self-gravitating halos with decaying dark matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05474) <a id="link11"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05474

**Authors:** Anna Bencke, Marc Kamionkowski, Annika H. G. Peter, Juniper Morales

**Abstract:** We study the evolution of self-gravitating spherical dark matter halos composed of dark matter particles that decay to a slightly less massive particle and a massless particle. The small kick velocity imparted to the daughter particle upon decay heats the halo. If the kick velocity is small compared with the characteristic velocity, the halo remains bound but puffs out. We also consider, for the first time, decaying halos with anisotropic initial conditions and find non-negligible effects on the resulting density profile. To simulate the halo evolution, we utilize the recently developed \texttt{NSphere} code across a broad range of decay times and kick velocities. We train an emulator to provide a mapping between the decay parameters and the relaxed density profile. We discuss how the resulting halo-scale signatures complement existing cosmological constraints on decaying dark matter and can be tested by upcoming surveys.

**Novelty:** 6

## 12. [Living near filaments: How environment shapes age profiles in low-mass galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06632) <a id="link12"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06632

**Authors:** Elisa A. Tau, Diego Pallero, Rory Smith, Raúl Baier-Soto, Antonela Monachesi, Silvio Varela-Lavin

**Abstract:** U-shaped radial age profiles have recently been reported in low-mass galaxies. This type of profile differs from the usually observed positive radial age gradients of the low-mass regime. We aim to characterise the environment in which low-mass galaxies with U-shaped age profiles are more commonly found. We use the IllustrisTNG-50 hydrodynamical simulations to select a sample of galaxies with stellar masses ranging from $10^9 \, M_\odot$ to $10^{10} \, M_\odot$. Based on their radial age profiles, we classify the sample into galaxies with and without a U-shaped feature. We define a U-shaped profile as one where stellar ages decrease towards a local minimum and subsequently increase, requiring an age contrast of at least 0.4 Gyr on either side of the minimum relative to its value. We find 145 galaxies with U-shaped age profiles ($\sim 11\%$ of the sample). They are mostly central systems ($\sim 72\%$) and $\sim 82\%$ of them reside within low-mass halos ($M_{200} < 10^{12} \, M_\odot$). We also find that galaxies exhibiting this feature are located at large distances from galaxy groups but close to cosmic filaments, with a median distance of $\sim 265$ kpc, whereas those without U-shaped age profiles tend to be located farther away from these structures ($\sim 351$ kpc).

**Abstract:** {Rapid rotation distorts massive stars, making parameters from spherical models inclination-dependent. Roche critical rotation requires $\eta_{\rm e}=1$ and $R_{\rm e}/R_{\rm p}=1.5$: despite the high angular rate, the central solution is not critical in equatorial force balance. The projected velocity exceeds, but is statistically consistent with, the previous spherical-fit value.} {\object{LAMOST J040643.69+542347.8} has a markedly oblate, gravity-darkened photosphere. Its proximity to critical rotation should be assessed using $\Omega/\Omega_{\rm c}$, $v_{\rm e}/v_{\rm c}$, and $\eta_{\rm e}$ together. A controlled spherical fit with the same atmosphere grid is needed to isolate the bias from neglecting deformation and gravity darkening.}

## 14. [A brief overview of the baryon cycle in galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06264) <a id="link14"></a>

**Abstract:** Galaxy formation and evolution is governed by the continuous exchange of gas between the cosmic web, the circumgalactic medium (CGM), and the interstellar medium (ISM) of galaxies. This brief review summarises our current understanding of this baryon cycle, from the accretion of gas onto galaxies to its conversion into stars, and the feedback processes that regulate the growth of galaxies. Several open questions will be highlighted, that will encourage progresses in cosmological simulations, multiwavelength observations, and theory to resolve.

**Abstract:** The role of active galactic nuclei (AGNs) in the suppression of star formation and structural evolution of galaxies during their transition from star-forming to quiescent systems remains unclear. We analyze 3,955 AGNs selected through five multiwavelength diagnostics at $010^{10.5}$) generally exhibit sizes comparable to those of SFGs despite suppressed star formation. Toward lower redshifts, they increasingly approach the QG size-mass relation, accompanied by declining specific star formation rates (sSFRs) and increasing S\'ersic indices. In contrast, lower-mass AGN hosts remain close to the SFG relation across the redshift range. Overall, AGN hosts exhibit relatively mild size evolution, following $R_e\propto(1+z)^{-0.6}$ over $0<4$, shallower than that of typical SFGs and QGs. We further examine the relationship between star formation activity and structural compactness in the $\Delta\log\mathrm{SFR}_{\mathrm{MS}}$-$\Delta\Sigma_e$ plane. At $z2.2$, however, some AGN hosts exhibit suppressed star formation while retaining relatively extended structures. These findings suggest that some AGN hosts may follow alternative evolutionary pathways in which star formation suppression precedes substantial structural compaction.

## 16. [A pocket of oxygen in a near-pristine galaxy at z=4.2: chemical inhomogeneity at 10-parsec scales in LAP2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05487) <a id="link16"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05487

**Authors:** M. Messa, E. Vanzella, S. Rojas-Ruiz, T. Treu, P. Bergamini, G. Di Rosa, M. Lombardi, P. Rosati, A. Zanella, A. Adamo, A. Bolamperti, F. Calura, M. Castellano, R. Gilli, C. Grillo, U. Meštrić

**Abstract:** We aim to constrain the oxygen abundance, the ionizing properties, and the spatial structure of LAP2, a strongly lensed (with a magnification $\mu$~50) oxygen-deficient galaxy at z=4.2 behind the galaxy cluster Abell 2744. We present new JWST/NIRSpec IFU PRISM observations (~13.6 hr) of LAP2, which we combine with archival JWST/NIRCam imaging. LAP2 is intrinsically extremely faint (M_UV=-12.7) and has a low stellar mass (M~$10^5~M_\odot$), comparable to that of massive star clusters. It consists of a compact core ($R_{eff}$~7 pc) embedded in a more diffuse emission (with $R_{eff}$~20 pc). Strong H$\alpha$ emission (EW$_{\rm rest}\simeq880$ \r{A}, $\log\xi_{\rm ion}\simeq25.3$), a blue UV slope ($\beta_{\rm UV}\simeq-2.5$, down to $\simeq-3.1$ in the core), and a Balmer decrement consistent with no dust attenuation indicate a young ($\lesssim10$ Myr), dust-free burst. [OIII]$\lambda$5007 is not detected in the integrated spectrum, implying [OIII]/H$\beta\leq0.61$ ($3\sigma$) and, using an extrapolated strong-line calibration, $12+\log({\rm O/H})\leq6.4$ ($\lesssim0.5\%\,Z_\odot$). This places LAP2 among the most metal-poor galaxies known. Faint [OIII] emission is, however, detected in the core (with $\rm4\sigma$ significance), where we derive $12+\log({\rm O/H})\simeq6.6$ ($\sim1\%\,Z_\odot$). This difference can be explained either by local self-enrichment, requiring only $\rm \sim0.2\,M_\odot$ of newly synthesized oxygen, or by a higher ionization parameter in the core. The upper limit on HeII$\lambda$1640 (EW$_{\rm rest}<39$ \r{A}, $3\sigma$) does not exclude a contribution from metal-free stars. LAP2 extends the census of spectroscopically confirmed EMPGs to star-cluster masses and to $M_{\rm UV}\approx-13$. Its location in a low-density region supports a scenario in which underdense environments preserve nearly pristine gas well after reionization.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 7

## 17. [Horndeski Gravity as an Effective Dark Energy Fluid in Cosmological N-body Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06330) <a id="link17"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06330

**Authors:** Vicente Pedreros Aballay

**Abstract:** Unraveling the nature of the cosmic expansion, driven by Dark Energy (DE), remains one of the key challenges in modern cosmology. Modified Gravity (MG) models provide an alternative to the standard cosmological constant paradigm. These models predict distinct signatures in the growth of structure, yet analytical predictions are challenging due to the highly non--linear equations involved.   To overcome this problem, we study MG as an effective DE fluid. To this end, we have incorporated linear DE perturbations into the N--body code $\texttt{CO}N\texttt{CEPT}$, which relies on the Einstein--Boltzmann solver $\texttt{CLASS}$. We replaced the $\texttt{CLASS}$ version used by $\texttt{CO}N\texttt{CEPT}$ with $\texttt{mochi\_CLASS}$, a patched version that includes Horndeski family models. By writing linear DE perturbations ($\delta\rho_{\rm DE}$, $\delta P_{\rm DE}$, $\theta_{\rm DE}$, and $\sigma_{\rm DE}$) in terms of metric perturbations in the synchronous gauge, the N--body code is able to evolve the effective fluid using the Boltzmann equation. Our current simulations encompass a range of cosmological scenarios ---$w_0w_a$CDM, Hu--Sawicki $f(R)$ and cubic Galileon--- each extended to include massive neutrinos.   For each of the models, we have found deviations from General Relativity close to what is predicted in the linear regime: a sub-$0.2\%$ relative difference for $\Lambda$CDM, up to $40\%$ for $f(R)$ and a sub-$25\%$ for cubic Galileon. Since our code is the first to implement Horndeski gravity into an N--body code, its validation is a non--trivial computational task. Our novel code opens the door to explore a vast number of modified gravity models in the context of current and future cosmological surveys.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 18. [Cosmological Backreaction and the Separate Universe Conjecture: Dynamical volume partitioning of Newtonian cosmologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06787) <a id="link18"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06787

**Authors:** Thomas Buchert, Jan J. Ostrowski, Alfred Bovon

**Abstract:** We provide a description of general Newtonian cosmologies for collisionless matter in terms of an exact and dynamical volume partitioning of global realizations of Newtonian models. Employing the formalism for the description of cosmological backreaction, an interaction between the inhomogeneities of the formation of structures in the Universe and their average properties can be analyzed. Our results can be regarded as a tool for many potential applications in the context of Newtonian simulations, but they also provide an exact way to assume analytical properties of the universe models on regional scales in conformity with global integral constraints on their average properties. We discuss approaches in relation to `separate Universe conjectures' in the literature and examine their consistency with the exact partitioning framework. In particular, using the Average Expansion Rate Approximation (AvERA) as an example, we show that identifying locally assigned Friedmannian rates with physical regional expansion rates is constrained by the global Newtonian 3-torus condition. Alternatively, when the simulation cells are interpreted as non-material control volumes, Reynolds transport introduces mass flux and flux-backreaction terms, so that their dynamics is not in general that of independent Friedmann domains. We also discuss the corresponding consistency conditions for stable clustering prescriptions.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 19. [Brewing another binary in the Teapot: Nandou=Phi Sagittarii](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05455) <a id="link19"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05455

**Authors:** Idel Waisberg, Boaz Katz

**Abstract:** The iconic Teapot asterism in Sagittarius is particularly rich in intermediate-mass stars in multiple systems. Here we combine VLTI/GRAVITY, archival spectroscopic observations, past radial velocity measurements and photometry to solve for the orbital and stellar parameters of the binary Nandou=Phi Sagittarii. It has a period $P=238.844\pm0.011$ days, a semi-major axis $a=1.32\pm0.10\text{ au}$ and eccentricity $e=0.418\pm0.004$. The precision in the dynamical masses $M_A=3.8\pm0.9 M_{\odot}$ and $M_B=1.6\pm0.4 M_{\odot}$ is currently limited by the parallax $p=12.5\pm1.0 \text{ mas}$. We find that the original Hipparcos 1997 Intermediate Astrometric Data (IAD) are consistent with the expected photocenter semi-major axis $a_{\mathrm{phot}} \sim 5 \text{ mas}$, while the IAD from the Hipparcos 2007 new reduction yield a too small $a_{\mathrm{phot}} = 1.7\pm0.3 \text{ mas}$, confirming the suspicion that a too aggressive modeling of the satellite attitude can lead to overfitting that suppresses the orbital motion. Matching the primary's temperature $T_{\mathrm{eff,A}} = 12000\pm250 \text{ K}$ and radius $R_{\mathrm{A}} = 5.1\pm 0.4 R_{\odot}$ to stellar evolutionary models implies that it is a $M_A \simeq 4 M_{\odot}$ star near the end of the main-sequence. Nandou will eventually most likely become a symbiotic star.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

Venhola (Space physics and astronomy research unit, University of Oulu, Pentti Kaiteran katu 1, FI-90014 Oulu, Finland), G. Verza (International Centre for Theoretical Physics, Center for Computational Astrophysics, Flatiron Institute, 162 5th Avenue, 10010, New York, NY, USA), S. Vinciguerra (Aix-Marseille Université, CNRS, CNES, LAM, Marseille, France), M. von Wietersheim-Kramsta (Department of Physics, Institute for Computational Cosmology, Durham University, South Road, Durham, DH1 3LE, UK, Department of Physics, Centre for Extragalactic Astronomy, Durham University, South Road, Durham, DH1 3LE, UK), N. A. Walton (Institute of Astronomy, University of Cambridge, Madingley Road, Cambridge CB3 0HA, UK), L. Wang (SRON Netherlands Institute for Space Research, Landleven 12, 9747 AD, Groningen, The Netherlands, Kapteyn Astronomical Institute, University of Groningen, PO Box 800, 9700 AV Groningen, The Netherlands), A. H. Wright (Ruhr University Bochum, Faculty of Physics and Astronomy, Astronomical Institute)

**Abstract:** The Euclid mission will revolutionize our understanding of the visible Universe by collecting images and spectra of millions of galaxies across cosmic time and environments. Here, we forecast the number counts of galaxies emitting [OII], H\b{eta}, [OIII], H{\alpha} and Pa\b{eta} lines in the Euclid Wide Survey (EWS) and Deep Survey (EDS). We compare predictions from different models, including semi-analytic models and empirical scaling relations. We then simulate spectra for galaxies in the MAMBO lightcone using FastSpec, designed to rapidly generate one-dimensional spectra as observed by Euclid. We process these simulated spectra with the official Euclid spectroscopic pipeline to recover redshifts and line fluxes and derive expected counts. Predictions for H{\alpha}+[NII] emitters are broadly consistent across mocks and with existing observations. In the EWS, relative RMS scatter among mock predictions for integrated counts is approximately 19% for H{\alpha}+[NII], 44% for [OII] and 41% for [OIII], reflecting greater model dependence for oxygen-line forecasts. After accounting for flux losses and redshift misidentification, corrected flux measurements recover most intrinsic line counts, especially in the EDS. From the MAMBO-based FastSpec simulations, we predict that Euclid will detect about 42 million H{\alpha} emitters in the EWS and 1.7 million in the EDS. The same simulations predict approximately 0.5 and 0.8 million [OII] and [OIII] emitters, respectively, in the EDS, and about 1.3 and 7.5 million in the EWS. These non-H{\alpha} forecasts remain subject to substantial model-dependent uncertainties, as shown by scatter among the different mocks. These samples will be instrumental in pushing large-scale structure and baryon acoustic oscillation studies beyond redshift z~2, extending Euclid cosmological and galaxy evolution studies beyond what is achievable with H{\alpha} alone.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 21. [Primary and Secondary Star Formation in the Spiral Waves of NGC 628](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06516) <a id="link21"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06516

**Authors:** Bruce G. Elmegreen, Sean T. Linden, Kelsey E. Johnson, Angela Adamo, Daniela Calzetti, Varun Bajaj, Giacomo Bortolini, Matteo Correnti, Ana Duarte-Cabral, Kathryn Grasha, Goran Ostlin, Alex Pedrini, Jenna Ryon, Linda J. Smith

**Abstract:** To determine the role of spiral arms and stellar feedback on the formation of stars, we examine the positions of optically identified and emerging star clusters in NGC 628. We find that the youngest clusters extend further into the spiral arms at small radii than at large radii, as expected when the angular flow rate through a wave-like arm increases with greater distance from corotation. The embedded or emerging star clusters (eYSCs) are identified relative to four distinct morphological features in the 7.7 micron image: spiral arm filaments, spiral arm spurs, spiral interarm feathers, and bubbles. Spiral arm-related eYSCs are presumably first-generation stars made from interstellar gas that was directly compressed by the density wave, while clusters along the rims of bubbles are secondary generations produced by feedback compression. The mass functions of the first and second generations differ: eYSCs in spiral arm filaments extend to significantly higher masses than eYSCs in bubbles. Secondary star formation continues downstream from primary star formation, removing the expected age gradient through the arms. Bubble eYSCs account for approximately one-third of all the emerging cluster mass. The outer arms have a more mixed distribution of spiral- and bubble-related eYSCs than the inner arms, potentially providing a way to distinguish wave and material arms and to find corotation in wave-like arms. Among the 17 most massive clusters with log M(Msun)>4, eight of them are in four tight pairs; in at least one case, the dynamical time for the pair is comparable to the cluster ages.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 22. [Investigating the initial conditions of globular clusters via $N$-body simulations: I. half-mass radius and stellar density](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06648) <a id="link22"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06648

**Authors:** Ruoyun Huang, Baitian Tang, Long Wang, Holger Baumgardt, Xia Li, Genhao Liu, Jundan Nie, Hao Tian

**Abstract:** Born out of the turbulent, dense gas of the early Universe, globular clusters (GCs) represent a significantly different star-formation mode compared to the local Universe. To quantify the initial cluster conditions, we perform the long-term dynamical evolution of GCs using the high-precision direct N-body simulation code PeTar. As the first step in a series of studies, we investigate the two low-mass outer-halo GCs, Eridanus and Whiting 1. By comparing simulated number density profiles with observations, we derive best-fit initial half-mass radii of $2.0 \pm 1.0$ pc for Eridanus and $2.5 \pm 0.5$ pc for Whiting 1, corresponding to initial surface densities of $2592.03 M_\odot pc^{-2}$ and $226.35 M_\odot pc^{-2}$, respectively -- 2-3 orders of magnitude higher than their present-day values. The absence of multiple populations (MPs) in Whiting 1 and young massive clusters (YMCs), in contrast to the MP-hosting Eridanus, supports the scenario that MP formation requires higher initial surface density and/or lower metallicity. Using the simulations as a template, we identify five high-probability extra-tidal candidates of Whiting 1 from Gaia DR3 and APOGEE DR17, which exhibit coherent kinematics and chemical abundances. Our results demonstrate that the diffuse states of low-mass GCs are the product of long-term dynamical expansion and tidal stripping, and that their primordial configurations were significantly denser.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 23. [Quiescent galaxies at cosmic noon: from individual element abundances to star formation histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06331) <a id="link23"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06331

**Authors:** M. Longhetti, F. R. Ditrani, D. Bevacqua, G. De Lucia, A. Gallazzi, A. Gargiulo, F. La Barbera, C. Maraston, D. Thomas, P. Franzetti, C. Mancini, L. Scholz-Díaz, S. Zibetti

**Abstract:** Understanding the formation and early evolution of massive quiescent galaxies is crucial for a complete picture of galaxy formation in a cosmological context. Such a comprehensive picture can be obtained through measurements of individual elemental abundances, which encode the timescales and physical conditions of their star-formation and assembly histories. While extensive work at low and intermediate redshift has revealed strong correlations between stellar mass, metallicity, and abundance ratios, studies at z ~ 2-3 remain extremely limited. Their faintness and compactness make medium-resolution near-infrared spectroscopy for detailed chemical analysis extremely challenging. We present a science case to measure full elemental abundance patterns in a representative sample of massive quiescent galaxies at cosmic noon, exploiting rest-frame optical absorption features. We show that deriving robust stellar population constraints for these systems requires spectra with S/N ~ 20 per resolution element, together with a spatial resolution comparable to their ~ 0.1 arcsec sizes. We show that such requirements can only be met with a 40m class telescope equipped with a powerful adaptive optics system. In this context, SHARP-NEXUS, in combination with MORFEO-MCAO capabilities, emerges as the only viable ELT instrument capable of enabling this science, providing both the sensitivity and angular resolution needed. Although massive quiescent galaxies are rare at these redshifts, their study can efficiently exploit the instrument's multiplexing by targeting galaxy overdensities and coordinating with surveys of star-forming galaxies. Such synergistic strategies, proven feasible in high-redshift clusters and by multiplex-sharing facilities like 4MOST, would allow SHARP-NEXUS to open a new window on the chemical enrichment of the earliest massive galaxies.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 24. [Understanding galaxies and the origin of the golden mass through the study of scaling relations across cosmic history](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06333) <a id="link24"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06333

**Authors:** C. Tortora, A. Gallazzi, A. Gargiulo, F. La Barbera, N. R. Napolitano, R. Ragusa, S. Zibetti

**Abstract:** The total mass distribution in galaxies strongly influences star formation (SF), both directly via gas availability and feedback, and indirectly via the dark matter (DM) potential well. Star formation efficiency peaks at a characteristic stellar mass of $5 \times 10^{10} M_{\odot}$, often termed the "golden mass", where feedback mechanisms minimally affect SF. This scale also appears in other scaling relations: correlations between stellar population gradients, DM fraction, and total mass density slope versus stellar mass all show pronounced minima at the golden mass. How, why, and when does this golden mass emerge during galaxy evolution? Early cosmic epochs ($z > 1.5$) remain largely unexplored, yet they probe the formative stages of galaxy mass assembly. They provide the full redshift baseline needed to constrain the buildup of stellar mass and the efficiency of gas-to-star conversion. By combining high resolution, multiplexing, and a wide field of view, SHARP on the ELT will address these questions, delivering sharper and deeper data than JWST. In this SHARP White Paper contribution, we present a scientific case exploiting these unique characteristics. SHARP will enable precise measurements of stellar population gradients, DM fractions, and total mass density slopes. Using the mIFU mode, SHARP will obtain spatially-resolved stellar population and dynamical maps for galaxies at $z > 1.5$. Reconstructing these observables for early- and late-type systems will allow us to trace the emergence of the golden mass across cosmic time, and to decode the impact of galaxy processes (mergers, SN, and AGN feedback) by comparing observations with cosmological simulations. (abridged)

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

Wang (SRON Netherlands Institute for Space Research, Landleven 12, 9747 AD, Groningen, The Netherlands, Kapteyn Astronomical Institute, University of Groningen, PO Box 800, 9700 AV Groningen, The Netherlands), A. H. Wright (Ruhr University Bochum, Faculty of Physics and Astronomy, Astronomical Institute), H. W. Yeung (Institute for Astronomy, University of Edinburgh, Royal Observatory, Blackford Hill, Edinburgh EH9 3HJ, UK)

**Abstract:** The Euclid spectroscopic surveys will substantially increase the number of observed emission-line galaxies. We assess the ability of the Euclid Wide Survey (EWS) and Euclid Deep Survey (EDS), at the final DR3 depth, to recover the star-forming main sequence (MS), dust-mass relation (DMR), and mass-metallicity relation (MZR) from emission-line measurements. We use MAMBO (mocks with abundance matching in Bologna) spectroscopic simulations processed with the official Euclid pipelines. The MS is traced over $0\leq z\leq 4$ using Pa$\beta$, H$\alpha$, and [OII] as SFR tracers. The shallow EWS flux limit biases the MS towards higher normalisation and a flatter slope by selecting the brightest, most strongly star-forming galaxies. The deeper EDS enables almost complete sampling with Pa$\beta$ and H$\alpha$ at log$_{10}(M_*/M_\odot)\geq10.3$ over $0\leq z\leq 1.88$, with mild biases at both redshift limits. The [OII]-based MS is increasingly biased towards higher SFRs at the highest redshifts. The DMR is traced through the Balmer decrement over $1.45\leq z\leq 1.88$ and $0.9\leq z\leq 1.88$ in the EWS and EDS, respectively. The derived colour excess agree well with the input values, although detecting the faint H$\beta$ line restricts the sample to less obscured galaxies at fixed $M_*$, particularly in the EWS; the DMR is better sampled in the EDS. Finally, a golden sample detected in multiple optical rest-frame emission lines at $1.49\leq z\leq 1.88$ recovers gas-phase metallicities with typical accuracy of 0.1 dex at log$_{10}(M_*/M_\odot)\geq10$, while lower-mass galaxies show biases up to 0.15 dex. Relative to H-band limited sample, this sample preferentially traces the upper MS and lower MZR. Despite the simplified simulations lacking effects such as mutual spectral contamination and detector persistence, these forecasts provide a benchmark for future Euclid spectroscopic analyses.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 26. [Planting neutrino SE-EdS: A < 3% accurate clustering approximation for neutrinos and other hot dark matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06399) <a id="link26"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06399

**Authors:** Amol Upadhye, Yvonne Y. Y. Wong

**Abstract:** The age of the cosmic neutrino is upon us. Aside from their fundamental importance to particle physics and the recent tensions in their mass scale $M_\nu = \sum m_\nu$, neutrinos' degeneracies with other cosmological parameters in analyses of scale-dependent cosmic clustering will significantly affect the estimates of these parameters, making accurate, inexpensive, easily-implemented approximations essential tools for theoretical cosmology. We present a new linear approximation, SE-EdS, applicable to non-relativistic neutrinos and other hot dark matter, which is exact in the linear Einstein-de Sitter case, and < 3% accurate for a broad range of flat and non-flat cosmologies characterized by parameters $0.2 \leq \Omega_{m,0} \leq 1$, $0 \leq \Omega_{\nu,0} \leq 0.018$, $-0.1 \leq \Omega_{K,0} \leq 0.1$, and $w_0 \leq -0.6$, at all length scales. This broad coverage in $\Omega_{m,0}$ makes SE-EdS ideal for N-body simulations, whose redshift range $z \lesssim 100$ demands accuracy for time-dependent $\Omega_m$ between unity and the preferred late-time values $\sim 0.3$. SE-EdS may be used to paint neutrinos into simulations in Fourier space, where its < 3% error and neutrinos' $\leq$ 3% matter fraction guarantees < 0.1% errors in the total matter density contrast, thereby including neutrino effects upon clustering at negligible computational cost. We quantify the approximation's accuracy; extend it to a broad range of cosmologies; and explore its correspondence with higher-order corrections to existing linear response schemes. A simple implementation is available online at https://codeberg.org/upadhye/SE-EdS .

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 6

## 27. [Gravitational-Wave Microlensing as a Probe of Compact Object Population](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05947) <a id="link27"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05947

**Authors:** Eungwang Seo, Jose María Ezquiaga, Martin Hendry

**Abstract:** The abundance of compact objects ranging from stellar to intermediate masses ($\sim 1 - 10^{5}M_{\odot}$) remains poorly constrained, particularly for populations such as primordial black holes and dark matter subhalos that emit little or no electromagnetic radiation. Gravitational-wave (GW) microlensing provides a complementary way to probe these populations through wave-optics effects imprinted on the GW signals, with implications for astrophysical and cosmological inference. We develop a hierarchical Bayesian framework to infer the population properties of microlenses from an ensemble of microlensed GW events, constraining the slope of the power-law lens mass function ($\alpha_{\rm ML}$), the lens number density ($n_0$), and the fraction of dark matter in compact lenses ($f_{\rm DM}$). Using simulated microlensed GW signals drawn from a realistic binary black hole population, we find that $\sim 100$ detected microlensed events are able to constrain both $\alpha_{\rm ML}$ and $n_0$, although a degeneracy between the two remains. For a low-mass-dominated lens population ($\alpha_{\rm ML}=2$) with $n_0=10^6~\mathrm{Mpc}^{-3}$, a three-year observation at the O4 sensitivity yields $\sim 130$ detectable microlensed events and recovers the injected dark-matter fraction, $f_{\rm DM}\simeq 1.2 \times 10^{-4}$, with a 90% credible interval of $7.3 \times 10^{-5} \lesssim f_{\rm DM} \lesssim 1.8 \times 10^{-4}$.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 6

## 28. [Shining LIMelight into the dark: Probing Dark matter - baryon interactions with Line Intensity Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02311) <a id="link28"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.02311

**Authors:** Durba Ghosh, Ranjini Mondol, Ranjan Laha

**Abstract:** Line-intensity mapping (LIM) is an emerging technique for probing both the astrophysics of galaxy formation and the cosmological evolution of large-scale structure. We investigate, for the first time, its potential as a probe of dark matter-baryon (DM-$b$) interactions using the upcoming Carbon Monoxide Mapping Array Project (COMAP) surveys, COMAP-EoR (Epoch of Reionization) and COMAP-ERA (Expanded Reionization Array). Elastic scattering of dark matter (DM) with baryons, i.e., protons ($p$) and electrons ($e$), exchange momentum and heat with the DM, suppressing structure formation and leaving characteristic signatures in the linear matter power spectrum. These effects propagate to the LIM power spectrum (PS) and voxel intensity distribution (VID). We consider momentum-transfer cross sections $\sigma^{\chi b}_{T,n}=\sigma^{\chi b}_{n}v^n$, with $n=0,-2,$ and $-4$, corresponding to contact, electric dipole-moment-like, and Coulomb-like interactions, respectively. Using Fisher forecasts, we obtain the projected sensitivities to $\sigma^{\chi b}_{n}$ from the PS and VID. Our VID-only forecasts combining the 16 and 30 GHz observed bands yield stronger sensitivities than existing cosmological limits for $n=-2$ and $n=-4$ for sub-GeV DM masses. We also explore the potential of combining the PS and VID to probe these interactions. Our study highlights LIM as a complementary probe of DM models alongside other astrophysical, cosmological, and terrestrial searches.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 5

Vinciguerra (Aix-Marseille Université, CNRS, CNES, LAM, Marseille, France), M. von Wietersheim-Kramsta (Department of Physics, Institute for Computational Cosmology, Durham University, South Road, Durham, DH1 3LE, UK, Department of Physics, Centre for Extragalactic Astronomy, Durham University, South Road, Durham, DH1 3LE, UK), N. A. Walton (Institute of Astronomy, University of Cambridge, Madingley Road, Cambridge CB3 0HA, UK), L. Wang (SRON Netherlands Institute for Space Research, Landleven 12, 9747 AD, Groningen, The Netherlands, Kapteyn Astronomical Institute, University of Groningen, PO Box 800, 9700 AV Groningen, The Netherlands), A. H. Wright (Ruhr University Bochum, Faculty of Physics and Astronomy, Astronomical Institute), D. Scott (Department of Physics and Astronomy, University of British Columbia, Vancouver, BC V6T 1Z1, Canada)

**Abstract:** We demonstrate the efficacy of crowd-sourced refinement of strong gravitational lens candidate samples by citizen scientists. Binary (lens/non-lens) classification by citizen scientists has been conducted previously, and here we extend such categorisation into `definite', `probable', `possible', and `non-lenses'. This grading is a required stage ahead of any strong lens sample publication and is usually carried out by (small) teams of researchers. We compare the responses of the volunteers to those of strong lens researchers and discuss the optimal strategy for lens classification and grading in future strong lens searches. Volunteers are found to be able to correctly identify systems of each grade, and their responses can be calibrated to be comparable to those of researchers. Grade posteriors, defined as the probability that a strong lens system classified by the volunteers is a certain grade, converge most rapidly for non-lens systems. We find that one researcher classification is equivalent to only 1.9 volunteer classifications when grading such systems. There is significant disagreement in the grading of intermediate-grade lens candidates amongst both volunteers and researchers. Moving towards calibrated lens probabilities for future lens searches would help to account for such subjectivity. If the Q1 lens search was rerun with volunteers performing lens grading (and corresponding calibration), a completeness of 79% for Grades A+B (corresponding to definite + probable lenses respectively) could be achieved with 41% purity prior to researcher inspection - this would correspond to an expected 12500 true lenses (i.e. excluding contaminants) in Euclid DR1.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 5

## 30. [Evolutionary Map of the Universe (EMU): Selection of active galactic nuclei and star-formation emission with machine-learning techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06190) <a id="link30"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06190

**Authors:** R. Carvajal, I. Matute, J. Afonso, H. Messias, M. J. I. Brown, D. Farrah, A. M. Hopkins, J. Th. van Loon, K. J. Luken, C. M. Pennock, J. Prathap, S. S. Shabala, D. A. Leahy, S. F. Rahman, K. A. Pimbblet, T. Zafar

**Abstract:** Sources detected by recent large-area radio surveys require robust classification and characterisation. The Evolutionary Map of the Universe (EMU) is a prime example of machine-learning (ML) techniques efficiently enriching existing catalogues with classifications and derived physical properties that would be impractical to obtain from spectroscopy or colour selection alone. We aim to build a labelled sample of sources in the EMU pilot survey (EMU-PS, at 944 MHz, with rms ~ 30 uJy/beam over 270 deg2 in the southern sky) classified as being dominated by active galactic nuclei (AGNs) or non-AGN processes including star-formation. We will then use this sample to examine radio properties and redshift-dependent trends. We applied a pre-trained multi-stage ML pipeline to optical-to-infrared photometry of EMU-PS sources to derive classifications and photometric redshifts, which we used to compute additional radio properties, including luminosities. We increase the known number of AGNs and non-AGNs in the EMU-PS by more than a factor of 15 (recall 96 %) up to z ~ 5, and by nearly a factor of 100 (recall 94 %) for z > 1, respectively. In extrapolating these numbers for the full EMU survey, we expect to recover more than 3.4 million AGN candidates. A non-negligible low-redshift subsample of selected non-AGNs shows radio luminosities in excess of those expected from star formation alone, revealing detachment between photometric and radio classification. Radio-derived properties show expected trends for luminosities and a lack of evolution for in-band (800-1088 MHz) spectral indices. The ML-based pipeline provides a scalable way to produce large catalogues for the ongoing EMU survey and future Square Kilometre Array observations and reveals that multi-wavelength analysis can uncover radio populations and trends often missed by colour cuts or spectroscopy alone.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 31. [Searching for Signatures of i-process Nucleosynthesis in Metal-Rich, Lead-rich Barium Stars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05470) <a id="link31"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05470

**Authors:** Catherine Manea, Madeleine McKenzie, Gail Zasowski, Giulia C. Cinquegrana, Amaya Sinha, Sarah G. Kane, Jaden Levine, Ellie Mebius, Aleksandra Winkler

**Abstract:** The intermediate neutron-capture process ($i$-process) has been invoked to explain the peculiar compositions of carbon-enhanced metal-poor (CEMP) stars enriched in both slow- ($s$) and rapid- ($r$) neutron-capture elements. Recent literature has questioned its operation at higher metallicities, but observational evidence remains limited. We analyse Gemini/GHOST and McDonald/Tull spectra of six $\rm -0.7 < [Fe/H] < -0.1$ stars identified in GALAH by their extreme Ba and Eu (or Sm) compositions ($\gtrsim$10$\times$ solar). We also reanalyse archival spectra of two literature $i$-process candidates. We determine abundances in up to 42 elements (Li through Pb), $^{12}$C/$^{13}$C, and Mg isotopic ratios, and compare to literature barium and CEMP stars and multiple asymptotic giant branch (AGB) model grids. Three stars exhibit extreme [Pb/Fe] (+1.79$\pm$0.19 to +2.50$\pm$0.32 dex), and two extreme [Eu/Fe] (+1.10$\pm$0.11 to +1.30$\pm$0.11 dex), distinguishing them from typical metal-rich barium stars. Two of these three exhibit abundance patterns that are inconsistent with $s$-process models and best explained by existing $i$-process predictions. The remaining sample is either consistent with $s$-process enrichment or suggestive of atomic diffusion. Both literature candidates are well-fit by the $i$-process model, but conventional $s$-process enrichment cannot be excluded. Our findings reinforce that metal-rich AGB stars contribute to the production of elements classically attributed to the $r$-process. These results identify promising candidates for $i$-process enrichment near solar metallicity and motivate additional $i$-process models to understand the contribution of this nucleosynthetic process to the chemical evolution of the Milky Way disk.

## 32. [ReveaLLAGN 2: Exploring the Nuclear Gas and Stellar Kinematics of Nearby LLAGNs with JWST/NIRSpec IFU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05496) <a id="link32"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05496

**Authors:** Antoine Dumont, Nadine Neumayer, Anil C. Seth, Torsten Böker, Jonelle L. Walsh, Micheal Eracleous, Luis C. Ho, Kameron Goold, David Ohlson, Timothy A. Davis

**Abstract:** Low-luminosity active galactic nuclei (LLAGN) represent the most common mode of black hole accretion in the local Universe, yet their nuclear structure, stellar and gas kinematics remain poorly constrained at parsec scales. We investigate the nuclear regions of eight nearby representative LLAGN, the ReveaLLAGN sample: M87, M104, M94, M81, NGC1052, NGC4258, NGC5128 and NGC4395. Using JWST/NIRSpec data, we probe the stellar, molecular hydrogen, hydrogen recombination, and high ionisation coronal lines kinematics and their spatial distribution, exploring how detectability and spatial distributions of these lines vary across different Eddington ratios. The stellar LOSV maps reveal a slowly rotating disk in the nuclei of all our targets, characterised by an anti-correlation with the h3 Gauss-Hermite moment. Across the sample, the stellar velocity dispersion rises toward the central black hole. Only in M104 and M94 does the velocity dispersion display a more intricate pattern that departs from a simple disk configuration, indicating the presence of multiple kinematic components in these galaxies. Across the sample, the gas distribution and kinematics exhibit rotation that is frequently decoupled from the stellar rotation, suggesting that the stellar gravitational potential is decoupled from the current accretion state. The gas kinematics are often visually aligned with the radio jet, presenting broader velocity dispersions along the jet direction. Line-ratio maps indicate that the dominant excitation mechanism is photoionisation by the central AGN, with mechanical heating from shocks becoming important only at higher Eddington ratios. Across the sample, we detect coronal lines in all LLAGN when using nuclear-aperture spectra, and we can obtain spatially resolved kinematics only in NGC5128 and NGC4395, the highest Eddington ratio sources.

## 33. [ReveaLLAGN 3: Revealing the Infrared Spectral Energy Distributions of Low Luminosity AGN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06916) <a id="link33"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06916

**Authors:** David Ohlson, Anil C. Seth, Kameron Goold, Nischal Acharya, Torsten Böker, Antoine Dumont, Ruby Duncan, Michael Eracleous, Anja Feldmeier-Krause, Juan Antonio Fernández-Ontiveros, Elena Gallo, Andy D. Goulding, Jenny E. Greene, Luis C. Ho, Sera B. Markoff, Mallory Molina, Richard M. Plotkin, Almudena Prieto, Shobita Satyapal, Glenn van de Ven, Jonelle L. Walsh

**Abstract:** The spectral energy distributions of active galactic nuclei (AGN) provide valuable information on the emission mechanisms taking place in their inner regions. Here we use the James Webb Space Telescope to study the near and mid-infrared spectral energy distributions of seven nearby low-luminosity active galaxy nuclei from 1.7 to 24 $\mu m$. We cleanly separate the AGN spectrum by performing spatial fits of the two-dimensional images across all wavelengths. We then compare these clean unresolved spectra to aperture-based spectra and show that they effectively remove galaxy contamination. Comparing our infrared measurements to X-ray emission, we find that low-luminosity AGN have enhanced near-infrared emission relative to more luminous AGN, a trend that correlates with radio-loudness, while their mid-infrared-to-X-ray ratios are comparable. A majority of our targets show strong compact silicate emission suggesting the presence of warm dust on parsec scales in these systems. We also quantify the near- and mid-infrared power-law slopes and colors of low-luminosity AGN. In the near-infrared we find AGN of all luminosities have similar spectral slopes and colors, however in the mid-infrared, we find low-luminosity AGN typically have steeper slopes and redder colors than more luminous AGN. Both the strong silicate emission and steep mid-infrared slopes disagree with existing models for low-luminosity AGN spectral energy distributions.

## 34. [The impact of M dwarf stellar convection on exoplanetary atmospheric signatures. A 3D radiation-hydrodynamics simulation analysis for high-resolution spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05740) <a id="link34"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05740

**Authors:** A. Chiavassa, M. Brogi, T. Masseron, R. Van den Broeck, L. Bigot, V. Parmentier

**Abstract:** M dwarf stars represent prime targets for the detection and characterization of small, temperate exoplanets; however, their complex activity and convective surfaces pose significant challenges for high-resolution spectroscopic studies. This work aims to investigate the impact of stellar convection on high-resolution spectroscopic observations of M dwarf stars and develop an integrated approach combining 3D radiative hydrodynamical simulations, spectroscopic modeling, and transit analysis to mitigate stellar noise. We employ 3D radiative hydrodynamical simulations of M dwarf stellar convection to generate synthetic spectra. We compare the synthetic spectra to actual observations of GJ 3470. We model a high-resolution transit sequence of the Neptune-size GJ 3470 b, including centre-to-limb variations (CLVs) and the Rossiter-McLaughlin effect (RME), and process the sequence via principal component analysis (PCA) to de-trend stellar effects. Comparison of synthetic spectra with actual observations shows that current H2O line lists remain inaccurate, with modest agreement in line strength and position for the chosen K-band interval. During the transit of GJ 3470 b, the amplitude of CLVs+RME is comparable to exoplanet signals. Current PCA can in principle de-trend such effects, but its behaviour is highly dependent on the copresence of telluric and planetary signals. While we are able to correctly detect the exoplanet with no evident bias from stellar residuals, this result is limited to a narrow spectral interval and a specific orbital configuration. This study demonstrates that 3D radiative hydrodynamical simulations are essential for accurately modeling convection-induced spectral variability in M dwarfs. Our integrated framework establishes a foundation to obtain unbiased measurements from future ELT observations of exoplanets orbiting M dwarfs.

## 35. [CHEX-MATE: Density profile reconstruction in galaxy clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05476) <a id="link35"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05476

**Authors:** I. Bartalucci, G. W. Pratt, E. Rasia, M. Arnaud, R. T. Duffy, Y. E. Bahar, M. Balboni, E. Baraldi, R. M. Batalha, A. Bonafede, H. Bourdin, F. De Luca, M. De Petris, S. Ettori, A. Ferragamo, M. Gaspari, F. Gastaldello, M. Gitti, A. Iqbal, M. Lepore, S. T. Kay, L. Lovisari, P. Mazzotta, B. J. Maughan, F. Oppizzi, E. Pointecouteau, M. Rossetti, J. Sayers, M. Sereno

**Abstract:** We investigated the reconstruction of the intra-cluster medium radial density profiles in galaxy clusters, focusing on the impact of cluster morphology, dynamical state, and methodological choices, within the framework of the CHEX-MATE project. Using a sample of 109 simulated galaxy clusters analysed along 40 independent lines of sight, we compared azimuthal mean and median techniques for deprojection. We find that azimuthal median-based density profiles recover the true gas density with negligible bias over the radial range $[0.15-1.3] R_{500}$. Mean based profiles are affected by the presence of substructures. While an accurate masking of clumps or substructures can reduce the bias to $< 5\%$, the discrepancy increases substantially in disturbed systems or when substructures remain unresolved. The mean-to-median density ratio statistically traces projected gas inhomogeneities, although intrinsic scatter and projection effects limit its use for individual clusters. The complementary use of mean and median profiles therefore provides the most complete approach for large X-ray cluster samples.

## 36. [Unsupervised Neural Operators for Rotating Relativistic Stars: High-Fidelity Equation of State Inference and Pulsar Spin Evolution at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05790) <a id="link36"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.05790

**Authors:** Anarya Ray

**Abstract:** Observed populations of rotating neutron stars~(NSs) with millisecond-order periods are powerful probes of fundamental physics. Mass-radius measurements inform poorly constrained properties of degenerate nuclear matter, whereas high-fidelity population synthesis connects unknowns in NS formation and binary evolution to large radio-timing surveys. However, repeated structure calculations are prohibitive due to the computational cost of solving the highly coupled partial differential equations of axisymmetric general relativistic hydrostatic equilibrium that govern rotating NS interiors. Non-rotating calculations or approximate rotational corrections can bias equation of state inference and limit the fidelity of population synthesis in tracing the evolutionary history of progenitors and propagating equation of state uncertainties. In this \textit{Letter}, we develop physics-informed neural operators that can solve the axisymmetric problem with high accuracy and negligible computational cost. \textit{Our solvers are trained in a fully unsupervised setting, with the training objective informed solely by the partial differential equations, and achieve a $10^3\times$ speed-up with negligible accuracy loss}. These solvers enable scalable EoS inference that self-consistently accounts for rotation and repeated stellar-structure calculations during binary evolution, making spin predictions that account for rotational structure and EoS uncertainties computationally accessible to population synthesis.

Fortunato, Payam Ghafari, Ayush Hazarika, Vasiliki Karanasou, Trisha Khan, Yoelsy Leyva, Mahdi Najafi, Swayamtrupta Panda, Masroor C. Filippenko, Richard Stiskalek, Syed Ashraf Uddin, Duško Borka, Vesna Borka Jovanović, Youri Carloni, Umut Demirbozan, Predrag Jovanović, Mohammad Malekjani, Nils A. Nilsson, Saeed Pourojaghi, Jorge Venzor, Lilia Anguelova, Marika Asgari, Nihan Kat{\i}rc{\i}, María Pérez Garrote, Leandros Perivolaropoulos, Pradyumn Kumar Sahoo, Denitsa Staicova

**Abstract:** Tip of the red giant branch (TRGB)-based determinations of the Hubble constant ($H_0$), the current expansion rate of the Universe, depend on choices made in selecting and analyzing stellar photometry and in assembling the sample of calibrator galaxies hosting Type Ia supernovae (SNe Ia). The CosmoVerse Data Challenge invites participants to analyze public photometric catalogs within a shared distance-ladder framework, opening each step of the measurement to the community and testing how these choices propagate into $H_0$. Participants independently selected photometric catalogs, galaxies, and TRGB measurement settings, while using a common SN Ia compilation and fitting procedure to convert distances into $H_0$. Supported by public notebooks and a training school, the challenge received 26 submissions from 22 participants. All submissions used the same analysis notebook, many participants were trainees at the school, and the true galaxy distances are unknown; the results therefore characterize the sensitivity of the submissions to analysis and sample-selection choices and are not a combined determination of $H_0$ or an estimate of the systematic uncertainty in published TRGB measurements. The inferred $H_0$ values have a median of $77.10\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}$ and a standard deviation of $7.38\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}$ $(5.11\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}$ for the scaled median absolute deviation), $\sim 3.4$ $(\sim 2.4)$ times the typical quoted uncertainty of a submission. Differences in the measured tip of the anchor galaxy NGC 4258 add a coherent shift in the distance scale. The challenge shows how different choices within a common framework can produce substantially different values of $H_0$, and provides an open basis for tracing these differences and for making datasets and tools more transparent and accessible.

## 38. [Supermassive black holes and nuclear star clusters in galaxies: constraining formation and co-evolution with SHARP and MICADO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06334) <a id="link38"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06334

**Authors:** E. Dalla Bonta', E. Portaluri, C. Arcidiacono, E. Bortolas, S. Ciroi, E. M. Corsini, D. Gasparri, L. Morelli, A. Pizzella, M. Signorini

**Abstract:** Nuclear star clusters and supermassive black holes are the dominant compact components in galaxy nuclei. They can coexist within the same system, and their relative prominence traces the history of gas inflow, star formation, and dynamical evolution on parsec scales. Key questions remain open concerning the physical channels that assemble nuclear star clusters, the mass regimes in which supermassive black holes become dynamically dominant, and the conditions under which compact stellar nuclei are suppressed, destroyed, or replaced by cusp-like structures. We describe how near-infrared integral-field spectroscopy with SHARP on the Extremely Large Telescope, combined with high-resolution imaging from MICADO, enables quantitative tests of nuclear formation and evolution scenarios within a unified framework. The joint analysis of stellar dynamics, stellar populations, and nuclear structure allows black-hole masses, internal kinematics of nuclear star clusters, and signatures of gas inflow to be measured consistently within the same systems, and defines the mass and distance ranges where robust constraints can be obtained. Recent discoveries of compact active nuclei at high redshift indicate that efficient black-hole growth can occur in systems with weak or compact stellar components. Although nearby galaxies are not evolutionary analogues of early systems, the diversity of nuclear configurations accessible with SHARP and MICADO provides a local laboratory for isolating the physical mechanisms that regulate the coupled evolution of central black holes and nuclear stellar structures.

De Caprio, I. Di Antonio, B. Di Francesco, G. Di Rico, M. Fumana, C. Eredia, E. Portaluri, F. Vitali, J. M. Alcala', S. Bisogni, R. Bonito, E. Dalla Bonta', M. Dall'Ora, G. Fiorentino, A. R. Ripepi, V. Roccatagliata, E. Vanzella, G. Vietri, H. Wang

**Abstract:** The Extremely Large Telescopes (ELTs), with their large apertures and cutting-edge Multi-Conjugate Adaptive Optics (MCAO) systems, promise to deliver data sharper and deeper than even the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) across large fields. SHARP is a concept study for a near-IR (0.95--2.45 $\mu$m) spectrograph specifically designed to fully exploit the collecting area and angular resolution capabilities of the upcoming ESO's ELT. The instrument concept is driven by the goal of tackling the most important questions in astrophysics and cosmology, bridging the gap between the local and distant Universe. This requires versatility to accommodate diverse observational needs. SHARP (http://sharp.brera.inaf.it) is composed of two main units: NEXUS, a Multi-Object Spectrograph (MOS) optimized for detecting the faintest sources, and VESPER, a multi-object Integral Field Unit (multi-IFU) designed for brighter ones. This presentation provides an overview of the scientific design drivers, the solutions developed to meet them, and the resulting optical design that achieves the required performance.

## 40. [Object-independent evaluation of small-data astronomical image classifiers: architecture, representation, and acquisition shortcuts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06484) <a id="link40"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06484

**Authors:** Eraldo Pereira Marinho

**Abstract:** Reported performance in small astronomical image datasets can reflect object reuse, field-of-view differences, acquisition settings, and catalogue-derived properties as well as astronomical morphology. We designed \dataset{} to measure these effects in a four-class task comprising galaxies, globular clusters, nebulae, and open clusters. The dataset contains 370 catalogue-supported physical objects, each represented by three Digitised Sky Survey FITS bands, and uses a group-constrained split of 222 training, 75 validation, and 73 test objects. We compared a custom CNN--Transformer trained from scratch with ImageNet-pretrained ResNet-50 and ViT-B/16 under an adaptive full-field representation, a common angular field, and two central-region-masked controls, using three fixed seeds. In the one-pass object-independent test, mean balanced accuracy on the adaptive view was $0.773\pm0.014$, $0.874\pm0.054$, and $0.866\pm0.023$ for the custom model, ResNet-50, and ViT-B/16, respectively. No primary architecture contrast rejected its null after Holm correction; ResNet-50 and ViT-B/16 differed by 0.007 balanced-accuracy points (95\% cluster-bootstrap interval $[-0.048,0.062]$). Controls based on acquisition and catalogue metadata reached 0.721--0.735, and central-region-masked adaptive views reached 0.731--0.800. These results demonstrate generalisation to unseen physical objects, but also show that image context, acquisition settings, and catalogue-derived object properties carry substantial class information. In this regime, high accuracy cannot be interpreted as evidence that a classifier learned target morphology, and the comparison does not isolate architecture from pretraining.

## 41. [Magnetic Properties of Galactic Molecular Clouds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06792) <a id="link41"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06792

**Authors:** Philip C. Myers (Center for Astrophysics | Harvard and Smithsonian), Ian W. Stephens (Worcester State University), Simon Coudé (National Observatory of Japan), Nicole Karnath (Space Science Institute), Howard A. Smith (Center for Astrophysics | Harvard and Smithsonian)

**Abstract:** The earliest studies of molecular clouds (MCs) showed scaling relations between mass and radius M$\sim$R^s with exponent s $\approx$ 2, known as the third Larson relation. More recent CO surveys indicate 2 < s < 3 in the inner Milky Way (MW) and nearby galaxies. These observations appear consistent with the virial equilibrium (VE) of magnetically subcritical clouds, bound by the pressure Pe of the midplane ISM. The VE equations for such clouds are consistent with 2 < s < 3 for magnetically subcritical clouds, but not for magnetically critical, supercritical, or nonmagnetic clouds. We fit the subcritical VE equations to M-R trends observed in CO surveys of $\sim$1500 clouds, with correlation coefficients 0.8 - 1. The fits indicate median mass-to-flux ratio lambda $\approx$ 0.7, magnetic field strength Btot $\approx$ 60$\mathrm{\mu G}$, and Pe/k $\approx$ 5 x 10^5 cm^-3 K in the inner MW, with lesser values in M33 and M31. For 18 MW "bones," VE analysis assuming likely values of Pe also indicates subcritical values of lambda, as found from polarization observations. These results indicate that many giant molecular clouds (GMCs) are consistent with pressure-bound magnetically subcritical virial equilibrium. If so, GMCs may have low star formation efficiency (SFE) because their relatively strong fields cause filaments, clumps and cores to form slowly, while feedback and turbulence disperse them rapidly. The fields in their clumps and cores are likely weak enough to allow star-forming collapse, according to Zeeman and DCF observations, and according to MHD simulations which predict SFE $\approx$ 0.5-2%.

## 42. [Population synthesis of substellar companions surviving common-envelope evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06549) <a id="link42"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06549

**Authors:** Nicolas F. Miranda, Alex Mauch-Soriano, Joaquin A. Barraza-Jorquera, Matthias R. Schreiber, Maria Paula Ronco, Felipe Lagos-Vilches

**Abstract:** Predicting the population of substellar companions around white dwarfs (WDs) requires accounting for both companions that avoid engulfment during stellar evolution and those that survive common-envelope (CE) evolution. While the former population was investigated in our previous population-synthesis study, a comprehensive population synthesis of the latter remains lacking. We investigated the fate of engulfed substellar companions within the same population-synthesis framework. We aim to predict the occurrence rates and properties of companions surviving CE evolution, identify the conditions permitting survival, and assess their detectability. We determined the stellar structure at the onset of CE evolution and calculated CE outcomes for different ejection efficiencies considering gravitational and thermal energy or the full internal energy of the envelope. Survival is rare and strongly depends on the evolutionary stage at engulfment. No companions entering CE evolution during the red-giant branch or early asymptotic-giant branch survive. Survivors originate exclusively from the thermally pulsing asymptotic-giant branch, when stellar envelopes can become weakly bound, allowing even relatively low-mass companions to survive. The resulting systems typically have orbital periods of a few hours. We predict occurrence rates of up to 0.16% among WDs, corresponding to an upper limit of 2.53 companions to WDs within 50 pc. Transit detection is primarily limited by orbital geometry, whereas all predicted survivors would produce detectable infrared excess with JWST out to 50pc. We predict that CE evolution produces a small but observable population of close substellar companions to WDs. Survival is governed not only by companion mass but critically by the envelope structure at engulfment, particularly during thermal pulses, and by the contribution of recombination energy to envelope ejection.

## 43. [Magnetism among the oldest white dwarfs in the solar neighbourhood](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06548) <a id="link43"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.06548

**Authors:** Stefano Bagnulo, John D. Landstreet

**Abstract:** We present a new circular polarisation survey of approximately 100 white dwarfs selected as featureless DC stars and combine our measurements with previous observations. Our compilation contains 205 objects, of which 24 show firm circular polarisation detections, while another four show marginal signals that require confirmation. Four magnetic white dwarfs originally classified as DC show polarisation spectra with significant contributions from spectral lines. Depending on the treatment of these objects and of the marginal detections, the inferred fraction of magnetic objects in the DC selected sample lies between approximately 10 and 14%. Among objects without identifiable line contributions, the circular polarisation never exceeds approximately 2%, substantially below the levels observed in some hotter magnetic white dwarfs with prominent spectral features. This is unlikely a symptom of field decay, since in our sample we find no significant evidence for a dependence of magnetic incidence on cooling age, and magnetic fields are detected in stars with cooling ages exceeding 9 Gyr. Instead, strong spectral line opacity provides a natural mechanism for producing much larger polarisation amplitudes than the continuum. In the full sample considered here, the incidence of detectable magnetism increases with stellar mass. Most average-mass magnetic stars for which repeated observations are available show no clear polarimetric variability, whereas variability appears more common among the most massive objects.
